I know perfectly well I already looked. It does make the worry stop, at least for a bit. Even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be... What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brought me in today is... I find myself checking things over and over. The same boxes, my lists, the drawers around the condo. I just keep checking them before I can move on to whatever we're doing. \n\nI know perfectly well I already looked, but I do it anyway. It's getting to the point where my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be and get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and won't go away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I keep getting these... well, nagging thoughts. I'll start worrying that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box somewhere. \n\nI tell myself it's completely unlikely that I did, but... the thought just comes back anyway. It makes it very hard to just sit down and settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have a set routine or... a specific order I go in. I just keep looking through the boxes or the drawers until I feel satisfied that everything is there. It's not about doing it a certain way, I just need to make sure I didn't lose anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd use the word compelling. It's not like something is forcing me to do it against my will, or like an outside force. It's more just... I can't seem to relax until I go look. I just want that nagging worry to go away, so I check. It's more about just getting stuck. Like I said, I find myself checking the same boxes, my old lists, the drawers around the condo over and over before I can move on to whatever I need to do next. I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me, telling me to just leave it be, but I just have to look again before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or make you feel less worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does. Like I was just saying, that's really what brought me in... I get stuck checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over. It's the only way I can finally move on. It does make the worry stop, at least for a bit. Even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be... I still have to do it just to get that relief so I can move on to whatever's next.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't check these things, something bad will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a fear of a specific disaster or anything like that. I have to do it to get unstuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, that come into your mind when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's usually about my old teaching files or my tools. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important, or maybe I put a tool I'm going to need in the wrong box. I'll try to focus on something else, and I even tell myself it's really unlikely I did that, but the thought just keeps coming back. It makes it very hard to settle down and just relax.\n\nDoctor: Are your checking behaviors focused on preventing something specific from happening to you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I find myself checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can move on. I do it even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be. It's getting to the point where my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be and get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and won't go away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I keep getting these... well, nagging thoughts. I'll start worrying that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box somewhere. \n\nI tell myself it's completely unlikely that I did, but... the thought just comes back anyway. It makes it very hard to just sit down and settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have a set routine or... a specific order I go in. I just keep looking through the boxes or the drawers until I feel satisfied that everything is there. It's not about doing it a certain way, I just need to make sure I didn't lose anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd use the word compelling. It's not like something is forcing me to do it against my will, or like an outside force. It's more just... I can't seem to relax until I go look. I just want that nagging worry to go away, so I check. But no, I wouldn't say I feel compelled, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'm trying to prevent a specific disaster. I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me, telling me to just leave it be, but I just have to look again before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or make you feel less worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does. Like I was just saying, that's really what brought me in... I get stuck checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over. It's the only way I can finally move on. It does make the worry stop, at least for a bit. Even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be... I still have to do it just to get that relief so I can move on to whatever's next.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't check these things, something bad will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a fear of a specific disaster or anything like that. Like I mentioned, it's really just about getting stuck. I check those same boxes, my old lists, and the drawers around the condo over and over just so I can move on. I know I've already looked, and my wife is right there telling me to leave it be, but I just can't seem to stop myself from doing it. It's not about preventing something bad, it's just... I have to do it to get unstuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, that come into your mind when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I'll try to focus on something else, and I even tell myself it's really unlikely I did that, but the thought just keeps coming back. It makes it very hard to settle down and just relax.\n\nDoctor: Are your checking behaviors focused on preventing something specific from happening to you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's not about someone getting hurt or a specific disaster happening. What really brought me in is just that... I find myself checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can move on. I do it even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be. It's just a feeling of being stuck until I do it, not that I'm saving anyone from danger.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts interfering with your daily life, relationships, or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are. Unpacking the condo... well, it's taken much longer than it really should. And I've been putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do at the library. My hobbies, too, honestly. It's just that I feel like I can't move on to those other things until everything is... verified enough, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts cause you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's an overwhelming distress, really. It's more... frustrating, I suppose. It's annoying to feel stuck like that, to know I'm wasting time checking things when I could be doing something else. It's getting to the point where my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be and get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and won't go away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I keep getting these... well, nagging thoughts. I'll start worrying that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box somewhere. \n\nI tell myself it's completely unlikely that I did, but... the thought just comes back anyway. I know perfectly well I already looked. Like I was just saying, that's really what brought me in... I get stuck checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over. It's the only way I can finally move on. I still have to do it just to get that relief so I can move on to whatever's next.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't check these things, something bad will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a fear of a specific disaster or anything like that. I have to do it to get unstuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, that come into your mind when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's usually about my old teaching files or my tools. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important, or maybe I put a tool I'm going to need in the wrong box. It's just that I feel like I can't move on to those other things until everything is... verified enough, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts cause you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's an overwhelming distress, really. It's more... frustrating, I suppose. It's annoying to feel stuck like that, to know I'm wasting time checking things when I could be doing something else. It bothers me that I can't just let it go, but I wouldn't call it a severe panic or anything like that. Just a persistent, nagging irritation that gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent harm or danger to yourself or others when you check these things?\n\nPatient: No, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it really isn't about that. I'm not worried about anyone getting hurt or some kind of disaster happening. It's just... I just have to be absolutely sure the box or drawer is checked before I can walk away. I'm not trying to prevent any kind of danger at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I'm not worried about anyone getting hurt or some kind of disaster happening. It's just... I just have to be absolutely sure the box or drawer is checked before I can walk away. I'm not trying to prevent any kind of danger at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that checking these things makes you or others safer?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Like I was just saying a moment ago, it really has nothing to do with safety. I don't think checking a drawer or a box is going to stop a break-in or a fire or anything like that. It's just... well, it's just about knowing for certain that I looked. Safety doesn't really enter my mind at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn't use the word regret. It's not like I'm worried I'll miss something important and kick myself for it later. 

Like I was saying, there's no disaster waiting to happen. If I don't check, the only thing that happens is... well, I just can't stop thinking about the fact that I didn't check.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brought me in today is... I find myself checking things over and over. The same boxes, my lists, the drawers around the condo. I just keep checking them before I can move on to whatever we're doing. \n\nI know perfectly well I already looked, but I do it anyway. It's getting to the point where my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be and get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and won't go away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I keep getting these... well, nagging thoughts. I'll start worrying that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box somewhere. \n\nI tell myself it's completely unlikely that I did, but... the thought just comes back anyway. It makes it very hard to just sit down and settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have a set routine or... a specific order I go in. I just keep looking through the boxes or the drawers until I feel satisfied that everything is there. It's not about doing it a certain way, I just need to make sure I didn't lose anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd use the word compelling. It's not like something is forcing me to do it against my will, or like an outside force. It's more just... I can't seem to relax until I go look. I just want that nagging worry to go away, so I check. But no, I wouldn't say I feel compelled, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'm trying to prevent a specific disaster. It's more about just getting stuck. Like I said, I find myself checking the same boxes, my old lists, the drawers around the condo over and over before I can move on to whatever I need to do next. I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me, telling me to just leave it be, but I just have to look again before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or make you feel less worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does. Like I was just saying, that's really what brought me in... I get stuck checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over. It's the only way I can finally move on. It does make the worry stop, at least for a bit. Even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be... I still have to do it just to get that relief so I can move on to whatever's next.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't check these things, something bad will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a fear of a specific disaster or anything like that. Like I mentioned, it's really just about getting stuck. I check those same boxes, my old lists, and the drawers around the condo over and over just so I can move on. I know I've already looked, and my wife is right there telling me to leave it be, but I just can't seem to stop myself from doing it. It's not about preventing something bad, it's just... I have to do it to get unstuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, that come into your mind when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's usually about my old teaching files or my tools. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important, or maybe I put a tool I'm going to need in the wrong box. I'll try to focus on something else, and I even tell myself it's really unlikely I did that, but the thought just keeps coming back. It makes it very hard to settle down and just relax.\n\nDoctor: Are your checking behaviors focused on preventing something specific from happening to you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's not about someone getting hurt or a specific disaster happening. What really brought me in is just that... I find myself checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can move on. I do it even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be. It's just a feeling of being stuck until I do it, not that I'm saving anyone from danger.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts interfering with your daily life, relationships, or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are. Unpacking the condo... well, it's taken much longer than it really should. And I've been putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do at the library. My hobbies, too, honestly. It's just that I feel like I can't move on to those other things until everything is... verified enough, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts cause you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's an overwhelming distress, really. It's more... frustrating, I suppose. It's annoying to feel stuck like that, to know I'm wasting time checking things when I could be doing something else. It bothers me that I can't just let it go, but I wouldn't call it a severe panic or anything like that. Just a persistent, nagging irritation that gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent harm or danger to yourself or others when you check these things?\n\nPatient: No, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it really isn't about that. I'm not worried about anyone getting hurt or some kind of disaster happening. It's just... I just have to be absolutely sure the box or drawer is checked before I can walk away. I'm not trying to prevent any kind of danger at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that checking these things makes you or others safer?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Like I was just saying a moment ago, it really has nothing to do with safety. I don't think checking a drawer or a box is going to stop a break-in or a fire or anything like that. It's just... well, it's just about knowing for certain that I looked. Safety doesn't really enter my mind at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not checking these things would lead to a situation that you would regret?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't use the word regret. It's not like I'm worried I'll miss something important and kick myself for it later. \n\nLike I was saying, there's no disaster waiting to happen. If I don't check, the only thing that happens is... well, I just can't stop thinking about the fact that I didn't check. I wouldn't regret it, because there aren't any real consequences. It would just be highly irritating to sit there wondering, until I finally just went back and looked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's not like something is forcing me to do it against my will, or like an outside force. It's more just... I can't seem to relax until I go look. I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me, telling me to just leave it be, but I just have to look again before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or make you feel less worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does. Like I was just saying, that's really what brought me in... I get stuck checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over. It's the only way I can finally move on. I have to do it to get unstuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, that come into your mind when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's usually about my old teaching files or my tools. It makes it very hard to settle down and just relax.\n\nDoctor: Are your checking behaviors focused on preventing something specific from happening to you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really. No, not at all. Like I mentioned a minute ago, it really has nothing to do with preventing harm or anything bad happening.

It really is just... well, just the checking of those drawers and boxes that I get stuck on, not how the stuff inside them is arranged.

I don't have a set routine or... a specific order I go in. I just keep looking through the boxes or the drawers until I feel satisfied that everything is there. It's not about doing it a certain way, I just need to make sure I didn't lose anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd use the word compelling. It's not like something is forcing me to do it against my will, or like an outside force. It's more just... I can't seem to relax until I go look. I just want that nagging worry to go away, so I check. But no, I wouldn't say I feel compelled, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'm trying to prevent a specific disaster. It's more about just getting stuck. Like I said, I find myself checking the same boxes, my old lists, the drawers around the condo over and over before I can move on to whatever I need to do next. I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me, telling me to just leave it be, but I just have to look again before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or make you feel less worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does. Like I was just saying, that's really what brought me in... I get stuck checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over. It's the only way I can finally move on. It does make the worry stop, at least for a bit. Even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be... I still have to do it just to get that relief so I can move on to whatever's next.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't check these things, something bad will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a fear of a specific disaster or anything like that. Like I mentioned, it's really just about getting stuck. I check those same boxes, my old lists, and the drawers around the condo over and over just so I can move on. I know I've already looked, and my wife is right there telling me to leave it be, but I just can't seem to stop myself from doing it. It's not about preventing something bad, it's just... I have to do it to get unstuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, that come into your mind when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's usually about my old teaching files or my tools. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important, or maybe I put a tool I'm going to need in the wrong box. I'll try to focus on something else, and I even tell myself it's really unlikely I did that, but the thought just keeps coming back. It makes it very hard to settle down and just relax.\n\nDoctor: Are your checking behaviors focused on preventing something specific from happening to you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's not about someone getting hurt or a specific disaster happening. What really brought me in is just that... I find myself checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can move on. I do it even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be. It's just a feeling of being stuck until I do it, not that I'm saving anyone from danger.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts interfering with your daily life, relationships, or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are. Unpacking the condo... well, it's taken much longer than it really should. And I've been putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do at the library. My hobbies, too, honestly. It's just that I feel like I can't move on to those other things until everything is... verified enough, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts cause you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's an overwhelming distress, really. It's more... frustrating, I suppose. It's annoying to feel stuck like that, to know I'm wasting time checking things when I could be doing something else. It bothers me that I can't just let it go, but I wouldn't call it a severe panic or anything like that. Just a persistent, nagging irritation that gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent harm or danger to yourself or others when you check these things?\n\nPatient: No, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it really isn't about that. I'm not worried about anyone getting hurt or some kind of disaster happening. It's just... I just have to be absolutely sure the box or drawer is checked before I can walk away. I'm not trying to prevent any kind of danger at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that checking these things makes you or others safer?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Like I was just saying a moment ago, it really has nothing to do with safety. I don't think checking a drawer or a box is going to stop a break-in or a fire or anything like that. It's just... well, it's just about knowing for certain that I looked. Safety doesn't really enter my mind at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not checking these things would lead to a situation that you would regret?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't use the word regret. It's not like I'm worried I'll miss something important and kick myself for it later. \n\nLike I was saying, there's no disaster waiting to happen. If I don't check, the only thing that happens is... well, I just can't stop thinking about the fact that I didn't check. I wouldn't regret it, because there aren't any real consequences. It would just be highly irritating to sit there wondering, until I finally just went back and looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about certain things or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't experience anything like that. I don't have any specific words or phrases that I feel the need to repeat to myself. It really is just about physically looking at the drawers or boxes, like we've been talking about. I don't get stuck having to repeat sentences in my head or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things to prevent something specific from happening, like harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I mentioned a minute ago, it really has nothing to do with preventing harm or anything bad happening. \n\nWhat actually brought me in here is just that... well, I find myself checking the same boxes, lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can finally move on to whatever I'm doing. It's frustrating because I know perfectly well I already looked. But I just keep doing it, even when my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be so we can go. So, no, it's not to prevent anything specific, I just get stuck checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have that problem. Things don't need to be lined up or put in any special order. I mean, I like the condo to be reasonably tidy, but I'm not bothered if something is crooked or sitting in the wrong spot. It really is just... well, just the checking of those drawers and boxes that I get stuck on, not how the stuff inside them is arranged.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is driven by a need to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that at all. It's not about making sure things are perfect. I mean, I don't need everything to be flawless or exactly right. \n\nIt's really just... well, I just feel this urge to look again. I'll check the drawer, and it's not to make sure it's perfectly organized or anything like that, I just get stuck having to physically look at it one more time. So, no, perfection doesn't really have anything to do with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's not about doing it a certain way, I just need to make sure I didn't lose anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd use the word compelling. It's not like something is forcing me to do it against my will, or like an outside force. It's more just... I can't seem to relax until I go look. I just want that nagging worry to go away, so I check. But no, I wouldn't say I feel compelled, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'm trying to prevent a specific disaster. It's more about just getting stuck. Like I said, I find myself checking the same boxes, my old lists, the drawers around the condo over and over before I can move on to whatever I need to do next. I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me, telling me to just leave it be, but I just have to look again before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or make you feel less worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does. Like I was just saying, that's really what brought me in... I get stuck checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over. It's the only way I can finally move on. It does make the worry stop, at least for a bit. Even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be... I still have to do it just to get that relief so I can move on to whatever's next.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't check these things, something bad will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a fear of a specific disaster or anything like that. Like I mentioned, it's really just about getting stuck. I check those same boxes, my old lists, and the drawers around the condo over and over just so I can move on. I know I've already looked, and my wife is right there telling me to leave it be, but I just can't seem to stop myself from doing it. It's not about preventing something bad, it's just... I have to do it to get unstuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, that come into your mind when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's usually about my old teaching files or my tools. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important, or maybe I put a tool I'm going to need in the wrong box. I'll try to focus on something else, and I even tell myself it's really unlikely I did that, but the thought just keeps coming back. It makes it very hard to settle down and just relax.\n\nDoctor: Are your checking behaviors focused on preventing something specific from happening to you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's not about someone getting hurt or a specific disaster happening. What really brought me in is just that... I find myself checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can move on. I do it even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be. It's just a feeling of being stuck until I do it, not that I'm saving anyone from danger.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts interfering with your daily life, relationships, or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are. Unpacking the condo... well, it's taken much longer than it really should. And I've been putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do at the library. My hobbies, too, honestly. It's just that I feel like I can't move on to those other things until everything is... verified enough, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts cause you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's an overwhelming distress, really. It's more... frustrating, I suppose. It's annoying to feel stuck like that, to know I'm wasting time checking things when I could be doing something else. It bothers me that I can't just let it go, but I wouldn't call it a severe panic or anything like that. Just a persistent, nagging irritation that gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent harm or danger to yourself or others when you check these things?\n\nPatient: No, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it really isn't about that. I'm not worried about anyone getting hurt or some kind of disaster happening. It's just... I just have to be absolutely sure the box or drawer is checked before I can walk away. I'm not trying to prevent any kind of danger at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that checking these things makes you or others safer?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Like I was just saying a moment ago, it really has nothing to do with safety. I don't think checking a drawer or a box is going to stop a break-in or a fire or anything like that. It's just... well, it's just about knowing for certain that I looked. Safety doesn't really enter my mind at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not checking these things would lead to a situation that you would regret?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't use the word regret. It's not like I'm worried I'll miss something important and kick myself for it later. \n\nLike I was saying, there's no disaster waiting to happen. If I don't check, the only thing that happens is... well, I just can't stop thinking about the fact that I didn't check. I wouldn't regret it, because there aren't any real consequences. It would just be highly irritating to sit there wondering, until I finally just went back and looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about certain things or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't experience anything like that. I don't have any specific words or phrases that I feel the need to repeat to myself. It really is just about physically looking at the drawers or boxes, like we've been talking about. I don't get stuck having to repeat sentences in my head or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things to prevent something specific from happening, like harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I mentioned a minute ago, it really has nothing to do with preventing harm or anything bad happening. \n\nWhat actually brought me in here is just that... well, I find myself checking the same boxes, lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can finally move on to whatever I'm doing. It's frustrating because I know perfectly well I already looked. But I just keep doing it, even when my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be so we can go. So, no, it's not to prevent anything specific, I just get stuck checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have that problem. Things don't need to be lined up or put in any special order. I mean, I like the condo to be reasonably tidy, but I'm not bothered if something is crooked or sitting in the wrong spot. It really is just... well, just the checking of those drawers and boxes that I get stuck on, not how the stuff inside them is arranged.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is driven by a need to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that at all. It's not about making sure things are perfect. I mean, I don't need everything to be flawless or exactly right. \n\nIt's really just... well, I just feel this urge to look again. I'll check the drawer, and it's not to make sure it's perfectly organized or anything like that, I just get stuck having to physically look at it one more time. So, no, perfection doesn't really have anything to do with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that checking these things helps to calm you down or reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it calms me down. I don't really feel anxious to begin with, so there's nothing to calm. \n\nIt's just... well, like I said, it's just this urge to look again. If anything, doing it just makes me feel a bit frustrated because I know I don't need to, and it holds up my day. So, no, it's not about settling my nerves or anything like that. I just do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I'll start worrying that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a needed tool in the wrong box somewhere. \n\nI tell myself it's completely unlikely that I did, but... the thought just comes back anyway. It makes it very hard to just sit down and settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have a set routine or... a specific order I go in. I just keep looking through the boxes or the drawers until I feel satisfied that everything is there. It's not about doing it a certain way, I just need to make sure I didn't lose anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things, like something is compelling you to do it?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd use the word compelling. It's not like something is forcing me to do it against my will, or like an outside force. It's more just... I can't seem to relax until I go look. I just want that nagging worry to go away, so I check. But no, I wouldn't say I feel compelled, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'm trying to prevent a specific disaster. It's more about just getting stuck. Like I said, I find myself checking the same boxes, my old lists, the drawers around the condo over and over before I can move on to whatever I need to do next. I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me, telling me to just leave it be, but I just have to look again before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to reduce your anxiety or make you feel less worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does. Like I was just saying, that's really what brought me in... I get stuck checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over. It's the only way I can finally move on. It does make the worry stop, at least for a bit. Even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be... I still have to do it just to get that relief so I can move on to whatever's next.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't check these things, something bad will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a fear of a specific disaster or anything like that. Like I mentioned, it's really just about getting stuck. I check those same boxes, my old lists, and the drawers around the condo over and over just so I can move on. I know I've already looked, and my wife is right there telling me to leave it be, but I just can't seem to stop myself from doing it. It's not about preventing something bad, it's just... I have to do it to get unstuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, that come into your mind when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's usually about my old teaching files or my tools. I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important, or maybe I put a tool I'm going to need in the wrong box. I'll try to focus on something else, and I even tell myself it's really unlikely I did that, but the thought just keeps coming back. It makes it very hard to settle down and just relax.\n\nDoctor: Are your checking behaviors focused on preventing something specific from happening to you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's not about someone getting hurt or a specific disaster happening. What really brought me in is just that... I find myself checking those same boxes, my lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can move on. I do it even though I know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife is just standing there waiting for me to leave it be. It's just a feeling of being stuck until I do it, not that I'm saving anyone from danger.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts interfering with your daily life, relationships, or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are. Unpacking the condo... well, it's taken much longer than it really should. And I've been putting off my preparation for the tutoring I do at the library. My hobbies, too, honestly. It's just that I feel like I can't move on to those other things until everything is... verified enough, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these checking behaviors and nagging thoughts cause you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's an overwhelming distress, really. It's more... frustrating, I suppose. It's annoying to feel stuck like that, to know I'm wasting time checking things when I could be doing something else. It bothers me that I can't just let it go, but I wouldn't call it a severe panic or anything like that. Just a persistent, nagging irritation that gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent harm or danger to yourself or others when you check these things?\n\nPatient: No, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it really isn't about that. I'm not worried about anyone getting hurt or some kind of disaster happening. It's just... I just have to be absolutely sure the box or drawer is checked before I can walk away. I'm not trying to prevent any kind of danger at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that checking these things makes you or others safer?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Like I was just saying a moment ago, it really has nothing to do with safety. I don't think checking a drawer or a box is going to stop a break-in or a fire or anything like that. It's just... well, it's just about knowing for certain that I looked. Safety doesn't really enter my mind at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not checking these things would lead to a situation that you would regret?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't use the word regret. It's not like I'm worried I'll miss something important and kick myself for it later. \n\nLike I was saying, there's no disaster waiting to happen. If I don't check, the only thing that happens is... well, I just can't stop thinking about the fact that I didn't check. I wouldn't regret it, because there aren't any real consequences. It would just be highly irritating to sit there wondering, until I finally just went back and looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about certain things or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't experience anything like that. I don't have any specific words or phrases that I feel the need to repeat to myself. It really is just about physically looking at the drawers or boxes, like we've been talking about. I don't get stuck having to repeat sentences in my head or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things to prevent something specific from happening, like harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I mentioned a minute ago, it really has nothing to do with preventing harm or anything bad happening. \n\nWhat actually brought me in here is just that... well, I find myself checking the same boxes, lists, and the condo drawers over and over before I can finally move on to whatever I'm doing. It's frustrating because I know perfectly well I already looked. But I just keep doing it, even when my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be so we can go. So, no, it's not to prevent anything specific, I just get stuck checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have that problem. Things don't need to be lined up or put in any special order. I mean, I like the condo to be reasonably tidy, but I'm not bothered if something is crooked or sitting in the wrong spot. It really is just... well, just the checking of those drawers and boxes that I get stuck on, not how the stuff inside them is arranged.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is driven by a need to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that at all. It's not about making sure things are perfect. I mean, I don't need everything to be flawless or exactly right. \n\nIt's really just... well, I just feel this urge to look again. I'll check the drawer, and it's not to make sure it's perfectly organized or anything like that, I just get stuck having to physically look at it one more time. So, no, perfection doesn't really have anything to do with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that checking these things helps to calm you down or reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it calms me down. I don't really feel anxious to begin with, so there's nothing to calm. \n\nIt's just... well, like I said, it's just this urge to look again. If anything, doing it just makes me feel a bit frustrated because I know I don't need to, and it holds up my day. So, no, it's not about settling my nerves or anything like that. I just do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that not checking these things would make you feel like you're taking a risk?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like a risk. I don't think anything bad is going to happen if I don't look. There's no danger or... or consequence I'm worried about. \n\nIt's really just that physical urge to go back and check. If I try to walk away without doing it, I just feel this... this nagging pressure that I need to look one more time. But it's not because I think I'm risking anything. I know the drawer is fine. I just can't seem to walk away until I've checked it again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
